      **No convincing common-impact signal emerged under both noise models.** The shorter Pléiades windows select CL features near 00:43:33 and 00:47:10; importantly, the latter is an edge-censored tail, not a separately resolved event. The extended window returns to the feature near 00:49:41.

      The largest added correlation was approximately **0.282**, but was not exceptional after accounting for the full search.

      This does **not** reject the impact hypotheses: coverage remains incomplete, DG interference persists, and coupling is uncalibrated. These were timing-search reruns; the original energy/coupling assumptions remain unchanged.

      Both viewing pages are below 5 MB.
      The fresh **truth-separated localization rerun is complete**: it did not recover MH371. Scoring excluded the recorded track until evaluation. However, because we have previously studied this flight, it cannot honestly be called a *newly blinded experiment*.

      ### Proposed paper text — under 500 words

      WSPR (Weak Signal Propagation Reporter) records amateur-radio reception reports, including signal-to-noise ratio and frequency. Aircraft might perturb these signals, but ionospheric variability and transmitter/receiver behavior also do so. Consequently, intersecting radio-link great circles do not themselves establish aircraft detection.

      We tested whether anomalies preferentially occur near known flights, then whether they select accurate MH371 trajectories. Three methods were compared: the published 0.75-standard-deviation anomaly flag, frequency residuals corrected for shared station effects, and a background-calibrated combined signal/frequency score. The latter uses same-link medians and median absolute deviations—robust measures of typical level and spread—excluding the tested slot, adjusts for reported transmitter power, and estimates shared transmitter/receiver effects from other stations. Anomaly magnitude is calibrated against reports outside evaluation windows, producing centered evidence \(E_i\), not an aircraft probability.

      For candidate trajectory \(x\), packet-averaged exposure is

      \[
      q_i(x)=\sum_k w_k
      \exp\!\left[-\frac{d_{ik}(x)^2}{2\sigma^2}\right]
      \mathbf{1}\!\left[d_{ik}(x)\leq3\sigma\right],
      \]

      where \(d_{ik}\) is distance to the link’s great circle, five sampling weights sum to one, and \(\sigma=25\) km. With station-balancing weights \(a_i\), anomaly contrast is

      \[
      C(x)=
      \frac{\sum_i a_iq_iE_i}{\sum_i a_iq_i}
      -
      \frac{\sum_i a_i(1-q_i)E_i}{\sum_i a_i(1-q_i)}.
      \]

      Positive contrast means stronger anomalies near the candidate than elsewhere.

      For MH371, the combined score ranked 19/21 against time-shifted tracks and 38/49 against spatially displaced tracks, where rank 1 is strongest. Neither alternative produced positive near-track excess. Separately, 24,000 paths constrained by satellite timing/frequency measurements and flight dynamics were scored before revealing the recorded track. Truth reached only the 43.6th score percentile, weighted by baseline model probabilities. Selecting the highest-scoring 10% of baseline probability mass increased the weighted mean of whole-path root-mean-square errors from **150.8 to 291.2 km**. Allowing every candidate the same 44-choice feature/scale search left truth at the 50.2nd percentile.

      Assumptions include successfully decoded reports only, station-locator cell centres, interpolated aircraft motion, and permissive full-great-circle propagation. These localization runs exclude ionospheric ray tracing, verified antenna patterns, realized propagation modes, scattering amplitudes, and waveform phase/delay. Separate PHaRLAP screening retained too few effective exposures for strong inference. Dependent candidates and controls do not supply calibrated significance probabilities. This is a rerun of previously studied data, not newly blinded or WSPR-only global localization. Missing historical environmental-grid versions also limit complete bank regeneration.

      We found no demonstrated localization benefit under these models—not proof that HF aircraft sensing is impossible. Synthetic recovery does not establish real detection. Further work requires synchronized raw HF recordings, documented receiver behavior, independent aircraft truth, and genuinely held-out flights and receivers.

    import pathlib,json
    p=pathlib.Path('/jackbox/home/.iso/thread-storage/thr_rzreetwcyu/branching-filter-analysis');d=json.loads((p/'impact-context-inputs.json').read_text());print({k:v for k,v in d.items() if k in ['search','arc']});print([(x.name,round(sum(f.stat().st_size for f in x.rglob('*') if f.is_file())/1e6)) for x in p.iterdir() if x.is_dir() and ('refined' in x.name or 'pooled' in x.name)])
    PY
      """Pool independent saved SMC runs and report airborne geographic density.

      No inference, smoothing, or probability threshold is imposed by this report.
      Run weights are proportional to initial effort times estimated normalizers.
      """
      from __future__ import annotations

      import argparse
      import csv
      import datetime as dt
      import hashlib
      import io
      import json
      from pathlib import Path
      import shlex

      import matplotlib
      matplotlib.use('Agg')
      import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
      from matplotlib.colors import LogNorm
      from matplotlib.patches import Polygon
      import numpy as np

      A_KM = 6378.137
      E2 = (1 / 298.257223563) * (2 - 1 / 298.257223563)
      E = np.sqrt(E2)
      K0 = np.cos(np.deg2rad(35)) / np.sqrt(1 - E2 * np.sin(np.deg2rad(35)) ** 2)


      def digest(path):
          return hashlib.sha256(Path(path).read_bytes()).hexdigest()


      def q(phi):
          s = np.sin(phi)
          return (1 - E2) * (s / (1 - E2 * s * s) + np.arctanh(E * s) / E)


      def project(latitude, longitude):
          """WGS84 ellipsoidal cylindrical equal-area coordinates, kilometres."""
          return (A_KM * K0 * np.deg2rad(np.asarray(longitude) - 90),
                  A_KM * (q(np.deg2rad(latitude)) - q(np.deg2rad(-35))) / (2 * K0))


      def inverse(x, y):
          target = np.asarray(y) * 2 * K0 / A_KM + q(np.deg2rad(-35))
          lo, hi = np.full_like(target, -np.pi / 2), np.full_like(target, np.pi / 2)
          for _ in range(52):
              mid = (lo + hi) / 2
              less = q(mid) < target
              lo, hi = np.where(less, mid, lo), np.where(less, hi, mid)
          return np.rad2deg((lo + hi) / 2), np.rad2deg(np.asarray(x) / (A_KM * K0)) + 90


      def read_run(path):
          summary = json.loads((path / 'summary.json').read_text())
          config = json.loads((path / 'resolved-config.json').read_text())
          if 'sampling' in config:
              config['branching'] = config['sampling']  # archived report metadata key
          provenance = json.loads((path / 'provenance.json').read_text())
          if not summary.get('posterior_available'):
              raise ValueError(f'No completed estimate in {path}')
          if digest(path / 'posterior.csv') != summary['posterior_csv_sha256']:
              raise ValueError(f'Posterior checksum mismatch: {path}')
          with (path / 'posterior.csv').open() as f:
              rows = list(csv.DictReader(f))
          xy = np.array([[float(r[k]) for k in ['latitude_deg', 'longitude_deg']] for r in rows])
          w = np.array([float(r['weight']) for r in rows])
          if not np.all(np.isfinite(w)) or np.any(w < 0) or abs(w.sum() - 1) > 1e-8:
              raise ValueError('Invalid saved probability weights')
          w /= w.sum()
          return dict(path=path, summary=summary, config=config, provenance=provenance,
                      points=xy, weight=w, rows=rows,
                      initial_modes=np.array([r['initial_mode'] for r in rows]),
                      final_modes=np.array([r['final_mode'] for r in rows]))


      def physical_identity(run):
          cfg = run['config']
          model = json.loads(json.dumps(cfg['model']))
          model.pop('proposal_guide', None)
          model['dynamics'].pop('marginalize_manoeuvre_rate', None)
          return dict(model=model, inputs=run['provenance']['inputs'], modes=cfg['modes'],
                      mode_probabilities=cfg['mode_probabilities'], input_settings=cfg['inputs'],
                      scientific_source=run['provenance']['scientific_source_sha256'])


      def mix_weights(runs, equal=False):
          effort=np.array([r['summary']['initial_particles'] for r in runs],dtype=float)
          if not np.all(np.isfinite(effort)) or np.any(effort<=0):
              raise ValueError('Runs must record positive initial effort')
          logz = np.array([r['summary']['log_evidence'] for r in runs])
          if not np.all(np.isfinite(logz)):
              raise ValueError('Missing finite normalizer estimate')
          weights = np.ones(len(runs)) if equal else effort*np.exp(logz-logz.max())
          return weights / weights.sum()


      def pool(runs, equal=False):
          alpha = mix_weights(runs, equal)
          return (np.concatenate([r['points'] for r in runs]),
                  np.concatenate([r['weight'] * a for r, a in zip(runs, alpha)]))


      def quantiles(points, weights, ps=(.05, .5, .95)):
          out = []
          for col in range(2):
              order = np.argsort(points[:, col])
              cumulative = np.cumsum(weights[order])
              out.append(points[order, col][np.searchsorted(cumulative, ps).clip(max=len(order)-1)].tolist())
          return out


      def cdf_difference(first, second):
          out = []
          for col in range(2):
              grid = np.sort(np.r_[first[0][:, col], second[0][:, col]])
              cdfs = []
              for xy, w in [first, second]:
                  order = np.argsort(xy[:, col])
                  cdfs.append(np.r_[0, np.cumsum(w[order])][np.searchsorted(xy[order, col], grid, 'right')])
              out.append(float(np.max(np.abs(cdfs[0] - cdfs[1]))))
          return out


      def region_masses(points, weights):
          lat = points[:, 0]
          return {'south_of_35S': float(weights[lat < -35].sum()),
                  'between_35S_and_38S': float(weights[(lat >= -38) & (lat <= -35)].sum()),
                  'north_of_35S': float(weights[lat > -35].sum()),

      BRIEF PAGES 4
      Oxford AI Summit 2026  ·  1 
      OXFORD AI SUMMIT 2026 
      Agentic Design Studio : Design agents 
      that can earn trust 
      Participant assignment brief 
      Your assignment: Design agents that can earn trust 
      Design an agent whose workflow, tools, authority, guardrails and evidence can be explained and 
      defended. 
       
      1. Assignment at a glance 
      Choose one operational scenario and design how an AI agent should operate across the complete workflow. 
      The task is not to write persuasive prompt wording first. It is to make the architecture explicit: scope, 
      workflow, tools, state, rules, authority, exceptions, guardrails, operating loop and evidence. 
      Required outcome 
      A coherent Master Prompt with exactly 11 sections, supported by a Design Record containing your 
      stage answers, and Designer tables.  
       
      What you will practise 
      • 
      Separating deterministic automation, bounded agent judgement and accountable human decisions. 
      • 
      Defining tool permissions, authoritative sources and observable verification. 
      • 
      Designing escalation, recovery and layered guardrails before deployment. 
      • 
      Expressing success as testable criteria with evidence and failure conditions. 
      How to work 
      1. Login to the website https://oxford.summit2026.aparchai.ai/.  It uses ChatGPT account to authenticate.  
      2. Choose one scenario and keep it consistent across all 11 stages. 
      3. Answer the three essential questions in each stage in your own words. 
      4. Complete at least one meaningful row in each stage Designer table. 
      5. Review for completeness for both happy failure paths. 
      6. Assemble, inspect and review the final Master Prompt before submitting. 
      Oxford AI Summit 2026  ·  2 
      2. Choose one scenario 
      Code 
      Scenario 
      Operating context 
      Design focus 
      A 
      Employee IT Support 
      8,000 employees · 700 
      requests/day 
      Identity, device, access and technical requests within clear security 
      boundaries. 
      B 
      Supplier Onboarding 
      Hundreds of suppliers 
      each year 
      Verification, evidence, risk, compliance, approvals and vendor 
      records. 
      C 
      Travel Disruption 
      Resolution 
      Dynamic, time-sensitive 
      conditions 
      Delays, cancellations and missed connections while options keep 
      changing. 
      D 
      Invoice Exception 
      Handling 
      Thousands of supplier 
      invoices 
      Matching exceptions, payment risk and finance controls. 
      If using Custom Project, you may instead define a scenario using: scenario name; organisation and context; current problem; users and 
      stakeholders; existing process; known systems and information; known constraints; and desired business outcome. 
      Scenario discipline 
      • 
      Stay with the same scenario throughout the assignment. 
      • 
      Use enough operational detail to make choices testable. 
      • 
      If a fact, tool, permission or policy is not defined, label it as missing—do not invent it. 
      • 
      Treat the worked example as a separate learning aid, not as your participant scenario. 
      3. The 11-stage design method 
      Stage 
      Design focus 
      Prompt section produced 
      Why it matters 
      01 
      Understand Scenario 
      Agent Role, Goal and Operating Context 
      Establish a bounded purpose before deciding how the agent 
      should act. 
      02 
      Map Existing Workflow 
      Current Operating Environment and Process 
      Make the current work, hand-offs and completion evidence 
      visible. 
      03 
      Redesign Workflow 
      Role Within the End-to-End Workflow 
      Separate deterministic automation, agentic judgement and 
      accountable human work. 
      04 
      Define Tools 
      Available Information, Tools and Tool-use 
      Rules 
      Specify what the agent may observe or change and how 
      consequential actions are verified. 
      05 
      Design Memory & State 
      State and Memory 
      Keep persistent system state distinct from temporary 
      reasoning context. 
      06 
      Design Decision Rules 
      Decision and Operating Rules 
      Translate policy, evidence and authority into explicit 
      operating rules. 
      07 
      Design Exceptions 
      Exceptions, Failure Handling and Recovery 
      Design safe recovery before failures occur. 
      08 
      Human–AI Collaboration 
      Human Authority, Approval and Escalation 
      Make accountable authority and escalation decisions 
      unambiguous. 
      09 
      Guardrails 
      Guardrails, Permissions and Prohibited 
      Actions 
      Use layered controls so prompt wording is not the only safety 
      mechanism. 
      10 
      Build Prototype 
      Execution and Operating Loop 
      Turn the design into a repeatable observe–plan–act–verify 
      loop. 
      11 
      Evaluate Agent 
      Success Criteria, Evidence and Evaluation 
      Define observable success and failure before claiming the 
      agent works. 
      Completion rule 
      A stage is complete when all three essential questions have substantive answers and its Designer 
      contains at least one meaningful row. Optional questions deepen the design but do not replace the 
      essentials. 
       
      Oxford AI Summit 2026  ·  3 
      4. Working in the Site 
      Open the  Site(https://oxford.summit2026.aparchai.ai/) and sign in to chatgpt and agree to privacy notice. 
      The Home page shows your projects and their status. 
      1. Open Learning Guide for a professional tour of the workflow and controls. 
      2. Review the worked example to see the expected quality without copying its scenario. 
      3. Create a project and choose one scenario. 
      4. Use Continue to reopen the last saved stage. The stage rail shows all 11 stages and highlights the current 
      stage. 
      5. Record decisions in the questions and Designer table. Use Back to home or Exit whenever you need to 
      leave the project. 
      6. After all 11 stages are complete, open the Master Prompt area and perform the controlled ChatGPT 
      handoff. 
      7. Paste the final Master Prompt back into the Site, review the Design Record PDF and submit.  
      5. Controlled Master Prompt handoff 
      • 
      The Site packages the project name, selected scenario, answers and Designer rows from all 11 
      stages.Click Copy  and open ChatGPT.  
      Return and review 
      1. Review ChatGPT’s output against your recorded design. 
      2. Correct invented assumptions, vague authority and unsupported claims. 
      3. Paste the complete Master Prompt into the editable final-prompt field. 
      4. Make any final edits. Saving retains a new version. 
      6. What strong work looks like 
      Quality 
      Evidence in your work 
      Specificity 
      Named systems, roles, boundaries and evidence; no generic filler. 
      Bounded authority 
      Explicit autonomous, approval-required, human-only and prohibited actions. 
      Observable operation 
      Tool results and authoritative state prove outcomes. 
      Recoverability 
      Failures trigger safe retry, re-plan, escalation or stop. 
      Controlled decisions 
      Deterministic rules remain deterministic; human consequential decisions remain accountable. 
      Testability 
      Every criterion includes evidence and a failure condition. 
      Internal consistency 
      Workflow, tools, memory, rules and guardrails agree with one another. 
       
      7. Submission requirements 
      Submit only when all 11 stages are complete and the final Master Prompt is present. 
      Deliverable 
      Must contain 
      Master Prompt 
      One coherent operating prompt with exactly 11 required sections. 
      Oxford AI Summit 2026  ·  4 
      Deliverable 
      Must contain 
      Design Record PDF 
      Latest answers, Designer outputs, change history and final Master Prompt. 
      Final checklist 
      ☐ One scenario is used consistently. 
      ☐ All 33 essential questions have substantive answers. 
      ☐ Every Designer table has at least one meaningful row. 
      ☐ Human authority and prohibited actions are explicit. 
      ☐ Exceptions include safe recovery and stop conditions. 
      ☐ At least five success criteria include evidence and failure conditions. 
      ☐ The final prompt has been reviewed for invention and contradiction. 
      ☐ The Design Record PDF has been previewed. 
      ☐ The project has been submitted. 
      8. Practical controls and fallback 
      Control 
      Participant behaviour 
      Continue 
      Reopens the last saved stage without restarting the project. 
      Back to home / Exit 
      Returns to Projects without losing saved progress. 
      Cancel project 
      Marks the project cancelled; the organiser can see the cancelled status. 
      Versions 
      Prompt edits and submissions create retained snapshots for review. 
      Privacy 
      Participant work is private to the account and visible to authorised organisers. 
      Offline fallback 
      Use the Complete Offline Workbook; it mirrors all stages, questions and tables. 
      Work safely 
      Use realistic but non-sensitive data. If the Site becomes unavailable, continue in the offline workbook. 
      Keep the same project name and scenario so the work can later be transferred or reviewed 
      consistently.

      """Build the review manuscript figures from frozen scientific artifacts.

      No inference or scientific source file is modified. All new files live here.
      """
      from pathlib import Path
      import csv, hashlib, json, shutil, sys
      import numpy as np
      import matplotlib
      matplotlib.use('Agg')
      import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
      from matplotlib.lines import Line2D
      from matplotlib.patches import Patch

      HERE = Path(__file__).resolve().parent
      ROOT = HERE.parents[1]
      OWNER = Path('/jackbox/home/.iso/thread-storage/thr_rzreetwcyu/branching-filter-analysis')
      MIX = OWNER/'terminal-refined-mixture/mixed/model-mixture'
      sys.path.insert(0,str(ROOT/'crates/reporting/scripts'))
      import impact_context_report as mapping
      from arc_density_report import inverse

      def sha(p):
          h=hashlib.sha256()
          with Path(p).open('rb') as f:
              for b in iter(lambda:f.read(1024*1024),b''): h.update(b)
          return h.hexdigest()

      def figures():
          out=HERE/'figures'; out.mkdir(parents=True,exist_ok=True)
          frozen=HERE/'evidence/impact-mixture'; frozen.mkdir(parents=True,exist_ok=True)
          manifest=json.loads((MIX/'manifest.json').read_text())
          groups=[]
          for entry in manifest['completed']:
              source=Path(entry['path']); assert sha(source)==entry['sha256']
              dest=frozen/source.name
              shutil.copy2(source,dest)
              d=json.loads(dest.read_text())
              points=np.array([(p['latitude_deg'],p['longitude_deg']) for p in d['particles']])
              lookup={(r['evidence'],r['search']['id']):r for r in d['results']}
              for r in lookup.values():
                  w=np.asarray(r['mixture_weights']); assert np.isfinite(w).all() and (w>=0).all() and abs(w.sum()-1)<1e-10
              groups.append(dict(id=entry['id'],points=points,lookup=lookup,metadata=d['group']['metadata']))
          for name in ['manifest.json','report/summary.json','report/methods.md','report/source-ledger.md','report/independent-region-checks.csv','report/numerical-diagnostics.json']:
              shutil.copy2(MIX/name,frozen/Path(name).name)
          inputs=json.loads((OWNER/'impact-context-inputs.json').read_text())
          for k in ['search','arc']:
              p=Path(inputs[k]);shutil.copy2(p,frozen/p.name)
              inputs[k+'_geometry']=json.loads(p.read_text())
          inputs['search_geometry']['features']=[f for f in inputs['search_geometry']['features'] if f['properties'].get('atlas_layer')=='phase2_deep']
          mapping.CONTOUR_PROBABILITIES=(.5,.95,.99)
          plt.rcParams.update({'font.size':10,'pdf.fonttype':42,'svg.fonttype':'none'})
          families=['openap26-positive-lift-3-targets','openap2020-positive-lift-3-targets']
          labels=['OpenAP 2.6 polar','Published 2020 polar']
          conditions=[('flight-only','before-search','Flight and final contacts'),('nine-recoveries','before-search','Add nine recovered objects'),('nine-recoveries','atsb-d94','Add unsuccessful ATSB search')]
          fig,axes=plt.subplots(2,3,figsize=(10,6.7),sharex=True,sharey=True)
          for row,f in enumerate(families):
              for col,(e,s,title) in enumerate(conditions):
                  ax=axes[row,col]
                  for g in groups:
                      m=g['metadata']
                      if m['terminal_family']!=f: continue
                      p=g['points'][:,0]; w=np.asarray(g['lookup'][e,s]['mixture_weights']); order=np.argsort(p)
                      ax.step(p[order],100*np.cumsum(w[order]),where='post',color='#0072B2' if m['terminal_seed']==37097311 else '#D55E00',ls='-' if m['cowling_seed']==37093011 else '--',lw=1.3)
                  ax.set(xlim=(-42,-24),ylim=(0,100),title=title if row==0 else '',xlabel='Latitude (°N)' if row==1 else '',ylabel=labels[row]+'\nCumulative probability (%)' if col==0 else '')
                  ax.grid(alpha=.2)
          fig.legend([Line2D([],[],color=c,ls=ls) for c,ls in [('#0072B2','-'),('#0072B2','--'),('#D55E00','-'),('#D55E00','--')]],['Terminal A / drift A','Terminal A / drift B','Terminal B / drift A','Terminal B / drift B'],loc='lower center',ncol=2,fontsize=9)
          fig.tight_layout(rect=(0,.08,1,1));save(fig,out/'evidence-and-repeatability')
          # Four actual conditional residuals, not an average of aerodynamic families.
          fig,axes=plt.subplots(2,2,figsize=(9,9.3),sharex=True,sharey=True)
          rows=[]
          for row,f in enumerate(families):
              for col,seed in enumerate([37097311,37097312]):
                  g=next(g for g in groups if g['metadata']['terminal_family']==f and g['metadata']['terminal_seed']==seed and g['metadata']['cowling_seed']==37093011)
                  ax=axes[row,col];w=np.asarray(g['lookup']['nine-recoveries','atsb-d94']['mixture_weights'])
                  xe,ye,mass,regions=mapping.contour_grid(g['points'],w)
                  lat,lon=inverse((xe[1:]+xe[:-1])/2,(ye[1:]+ye[:-1])/2)
                  thresholds=[r['density_threshold_per_km2'] for r in reversed(regions)]
                  ax.set(xlim=(84,105),ylim=(-42,-24),title=labels[row]+' / terminal '+('A' if col==0 else 'B'))
                  mapping.context(ax,inputs,[],labels=False)
                  ax.contourf(lon,lat,mass/25,levels=thresholds+[mass.max()/25*1.01],colors=['#e6edf7','#b4c9e5','#6085b6'])
                  ax.contour(lon,lat,mass/25,levels=thresholds,colors='#264568',linewidths=[.6,.8,1.0])
                  for r in regions: rows.append(dict(group=g['id'],**r))
                  np.savez_compressed(out/(g['id']+'-display-grid.npz'),x_edges_km=xe,y_edges_km=ye,mass=mass)
          fig.legend([Patch(facecolor=c,label=l) for c,l in [('#6085b6','50%'),('#b4c9e5','95%'),('#e6edf7','99%')]]+[Line2D([],[],color='#087d4c',label='ATSB footprint'),Line2D([],[],color='#0072b2',ls='--',label='7th arc (reference altitude)')],['50%','95%','99%','ATSB footprint','7th arc (reference altitude)'],loc='lower center',ncol=3,fontsize=9)
          fig.tight_layout(rect=(0,.08,1,1));save(fig,out/'residual-50-95-99')
          with (out/'residual-contour-areas.csv').open('w') as f:
              writer=csv.DictWriter(f,fieldnames=list(rows[0]));writer.writeheader();writer.writerows(rows)
          summary=json.loads((frozen/'summary.json').read_text())
          selected=[r for r in summary['independent_region_comparisons'] if r['evidence']=='nine-recoveries' and r['search']=='atsb-d94' and r['cowling_seed']==37093011]
          fig,ax=plt.subplots(figsize=(9,4))
          x=np.arange(len(selected));a=[r['training_probability']*100 for r in selected];b=[r['independent_probability']*100 for r in selected]
          ax.bar(x-.18,a,.36,color='#0072B2',label='Run selecting the cells');ax.bar(x+.18,b,.36,color='#D55E00',label='Independent terminal run')
          for i,(v,z) in enumerate(zip(a,b)):
              ax.text(i-.18,v+1,f'{v:.1f}%',ha='center',fontsize=10);ax.text(i+.18,z+1,f'{z:.1f}%',ha='center',fontsize=10)
          ax.set(xticks=x,xticklabels=['OpenAP 2.6\nA → B','OpenAP 2.6\nB → A','2020 polar\nA → B','2020 polar\nB → A'],ylim=(0,80),ylabel='Probability in the same 7,500 km² (%)')
          ax.legend(loc='upper right',frameon=False);ax.grid(axis='y',alpha=.2);ax.set_axisbelow(True);fig.tight_layout();save(fig,out/'selected-area-instability')
          # Use published project figures unchanged; originals and captions remain in evidence.
          extras={
             'hydroacoustic-timing':ROOT/'.sources/kadri-2024-hydroacoustics/outputs/conditional-hypothesis-tests/expanded-timing-correlations.png',
             'wspr-control':ROOT/'.sources/godfrey-wspr-passive-radar/outputs/whole-track-trajectory-bank/publication/mh371-blind-wspr-trajectory-recovery.png',
             'gain-control':ROOT/'.sources/large-2019-antenna-gain/outputs/figure_6_gain_hypothesis_by_flight.png'}
          for name,p in extras.items():
              if p.exists():shutil.copy2(p,out/(name+'.png'))
          provenance={'source_manifest':str(MIX/'manifest.json'),'source_manifest_sha256':sha(MIX/'manifest.json'),'inputs':[{'path':str(MIX/e['id'])+'.json','sha256':e['sha256']} for e in manifest['completed']], 'new_inference':False,'display_cell_km':5,'display_gaussian_sigma_km':25,'probabilities':[.5,.95,.99],'areas_are_display_not_operational_search':True}
          (out/'provenance.json').write_text(json.dumps(provenance,indent=2)+'\n')
          print(json.dumps(rows,indent=2))

      def save(fig,stem):
          for ext in ['pdf','png','svg']:fig.savefig(stem.with_suffix('.'+ext),dpi=220,bbox_inches='tight')
          plt.close(fig)

      if __name__=='__main__':figures()

      red lines indicate the ﬂight paths prior to 00:19 UTC and the green lines indicate the
      potential additional ﬂight paths between the last signal at 00:19 UTC and the failure to
      respond to the LOI message sent by the GES at 01:15 UTC.
      It is important to remember that these initial ﬂight paths, while consistent with the
      BTO timing data and the aircraft performance, were based on a number of
      assumptions: that the aircraft travelled at a steady and high speed and did not make
      any manoeuvres beyond a turn to the north or south shortly after its last radar
      detection.
      4.
      FREQUENCY CALCULATION.
      While the timing work derived a
      series of high-resolution location arcs, these gave many different ﬂight path solutions
      resulting in an extremely large search area. Even discriminating between southern and
      northern routes was impossible, although the northern route appeared less likely given
      the large number of civil and military radar installations it passed over. The possibility
      also remained of a circular ﬂight path ending in China, Vietnam, Borneo or Indonesia.
      To try and resolve this ambiguity an analysis of the BFO data was undertaken, in the
      hope that the Doppler components of the two routes would be sufﬁciently different to
      discriminate between them.
      4.1.
      Theory.
      Unlike the timing calculation, which predicts the location of the
      aircraft relative to the satellite from the BTO measurement, the frequency calculation
      works backwards, taking the aircraft location and velocity at a given time and
      calculating the BFO that this would generate. This enables the likelihood of potential
      ﬂight paths to be evaluated, depending on how well the calculated BFO values align
      with the measured values during the ﬂight.
      The BFO may be calculated by combining the contributions of several factors
      illustrated in Figure 7:
      BFO = ΔFup + ΔFdown + δfcomp + δfsat + δfAFC + δfbias
      (2)
      where ΔFup is the Doppler on the signal passing from the aircraft to the satellite,
      ΔFdown is the Doppler on the signal passing from the satellite to the GES, δfcomp is
      the frequency compensation applied by the aircraft, δfsat is the variation in satellite
      Figure 7. Basis of Frequency Calculation.
      9

      NO. 1
      https://doi.org/10.1017/S037346331400068X Published online by Cambridge University Press

      .sources/ashton-2015-search-for-mh370/paper/paper.pdf PDFpage 10
      translation frequency, δfAFC is the frequency compensation applied by the GES receive
      chain and δfbias is a ﬁxed offset due to errors in the aircraft and satellite oscillators.
      The uplink and downlink Doppler may be calculated from the relative movement of
      the aircraft, satellite and GES using the signal frequencies of 1646·6525 MHz (uplink)
      and 3615·1525 MHz (downlink). The satellite location and velocity are accurately
      determined by Inmarsat for satellite station keeping and collision avoidance activities,

      ground speed of 450 knots, matching the BFO measurements to within 2 Hz at each of

      6.3.
      Consolidated Flight Path.
      It is possible to join the early and late ﬂight paths
      of the previous two sections to generate a hypothetical route that is consistent with the
      measured data, by assuming that MH370 ﬂew from the last primary radar ﬁx to the
      IGOGU air waypoint from where it turned south towards the ISBIX waypoint (N00
      22, E093 40·5) and then continued in a southerly direction with its true track drifting
      from the initial 186° (IGOGU to ISBIX) to 180° by the end of the ﬂight.
      Table 9 presents the results of the BFO calculation for the reconstructed ﬂight path,
      breaking out the different contributors to the frequency offset. The uplink Doppler
      contribution (ΔFup) is split into two components: aircraft velocity in the direction of
      the satellite and satellite velocity in the direction of the aircraft. The errors at 16:42,
      16:55 and 00:19 are thought to be due to vertical movement of the aircraft that is not
      included in this model. The values at 18:39 and 23:14 are based on the telephone call
      attempts, where BFO is recorded but BTO is not: the values in the table are the
      average of all the measurements associated with the call attempts, which spanned from
      86 to 90 Hz at 18:39 and from 216 to 222 Hz at 23:14. Figure 17 illustrates the route.

      3.1 The Problem and its Conceptual Solution
      13
      3.1
      The Problem and its Conceptual Solution
      To deﬁne the problem of nonlinear ﬁltering, let us introduce the state vector
      x(t) ∈Rn, where n is the dimension of the state vector. Here t is continuous-valued
      time. The state evolution is best described using a continuous-time stochastic differ-
      ential equation, sometimes speciﬁcally referred to as an Itô differential equation [23].
      However, it is often more convenient to sample this at discrete time instants, in which
      case xk ≡x (tk) represents the state at the kth discrete sample time. The elapsed time
      between samples k = tk −tk−1 is not necessarily constant. The state is assumed to
      evolve according to a continuous-time stochastic model:
      dx(t) = f (x(t), dv(t), t, dt) ,
      (3.4)
      where f(·) is a known, possibly nonlinear deterministic function of the state and v(t)
      is referred to as a process noise sequence, which caters for random disturbances in
      the aircraft motion.
      A sensor collects measurements, which are a possibly nonlinear function of the
      state. Measurements occur at times tk, for k = {1, 2, . . . K}. The kth measurement
      is denoted zk ∈Rm where m is the dimension of the measurement vector. The mea-
      surements are related to the state via the measurement equation:
      zk = hk (xk, wk) ,
      (3.5)
      where hk(·) is a known, possibly nonlinear function and wk is a measurement noise
      sequence. The noise sequences v(t) and wk will be assumed to be white, with known
      probability density functions and mutually independent. The initial state is assumed
      to have a known pdf p (x0) and also to be independent of noise sequences.
      We seek estimates of xk based on the sequence of all available measurements up to
      timetk,deﬁningthemeasurementhistoryZk ≜{z1, . . . zk}.FromaBayesianperspec-
      tive, the problem is to recursively construct the posterior pdf p (xk|Zk). In principle,
      the pdf p (xk|Zk) may be obtained recursively in two stages: prediction and update.
      The prediction stage steps from the pdf of x at time tk−1, p (xk−1|Zk−1), to the pdf
      at the next time, p (xk|Zk−1), not incorporating any new measurements. The update
      stage takes the predicted pdf p (xk|Zk−1) and incorporates the new measurement zk
      occurring at time tk to obtain the updated pdf p (xk|Zk). If there is a requirement
      to eval
      .sources/davey-2016-bayesian-search/paper/paper.pdf PDFpage 28
      14
      3
      The Bayesian Approach
      p (xk|Zk−1) =
      
      p (xk|xk−1, Zk−1) p (xk−1|Zk−1) dxk−1,
      =
      
      p (xk|xk−1) p (xk−1|Zk−1) dxk−1.
      (3.6)
      The ﬁrst line of (3.6) is a statement of the law of total probability. The sim-
      pliﬁcation p (xk|xk−1, Zk−1) = p (xk|xk−1) used to progress from the ﬁrst line of
      (3.6) to the second applies because (3.4) describes a Markov process of order one.
      The probabilistic model of the state evolution, p (xk|xk−1), is deﬁned by the system
      equation (3.4) and the known statistics of v(t).
      3.1.2
      Update
      At time tk a measurement zk becomes available and the update stage is carried out.
      This involves an update of the prediction (or prior) pdf via Bayes’ rule:
      p (xk|Zk) = p (xk|zk, Zk−1)
      = p (zk|xk, Zk−1) p (xk|Zk−1)
      p (zk|Zk−1)
      = p (zk|xk) p (xk|Zk−1)
      p (zk|Zk−1)
      ,
      (3.7)
      where conditional independence has been used to write the likelihood function
      p (zk|xk, Zk−1) = p (zk|xk), which is deﬁned by the measurement model (3.5) and
      the known statistics of wk. The normalizing constant on the denominator can be
      expanded as
      p (zk|Zk−1) =
      
      p (zk|xk) p (xk|Zk−1) dxk.
      (3.8)
      In the update stage (3.7), the measurement zk is used to modify the prior density to
      obtain the required posterior density of the current state.
      Note that there is no requirement for all of the measurements to have the same
      statistical model or even contain the same type of information. For example, there
      could be multiple sensors operating on different modalities. For simplicity, we have
      not introduced explicit notation to change the measurement pdf for each k. For the
      accident ﬂight three different types of measurement have been used. As discussed in
      Chap.5, the satellite communications messages consist of R-channel and C-channel
      messages that have differing information content. Another quite different form of
      measurement is the areas of the ocean ﬂoor that have been searched without locating
      the aircraft and the debris that has been recovered. This measurement and its potential
      use to reﬁne the ongoing search are discussed in Chap.11.

      .sources/davey-2016-bayesian-search/paper/paper.pdf PDFpage 73
      60
      8
      Particle Filter Implementation
      8.3
      Assumptions
      The key assumptions used by the ﬁlter are:
      1. The radar data provides an accurate estimate of the aircraft trajectory up to
      18:01:49. If, for example, the radar track used to build the prior were actually
      from a different aircraft, the predicted pdf would be invalid. Discarding the radar
      data leads to a signiﬁcant broadening of the search zone, and accident investiga-
      tors believe the radar data to be correctly associated with MH370. Chapter10.6
      considers an alternative analysis which ignores the radar data.
      2. The measurement error characteristics are known. The pdf of BTO and BFO
      measurements, in particular the standard deviation of each, is provided to the
      algorithm as a known input. Extensive study of the statistics of these measure-
      ments has been undertaken and the distributions assumed are well characterised,
      subject to the caveats discussed in Chap.5. Incremental changes, such as minor
      inﬂation of the assumed BTO variance would lead to incremental changes in the
      ﬁlter output.
      3. The aircraft cruises in one of ﬁve prescribed modes and does not change between
      them (other than a single possible change from lateral navigation to constant
      magnetic/true heading). It is possible that the whole ﬂight was continually under
      manual control but it is highly unlikely. The use of typical autopilot modes is
      reasonable.
      4. Inﬁnite fuel: the fuel constraints on the aircraft can be applied to the pdf after-
      wards. In the simplest case, maximum reachable ranges could be used to censor
      impossible trajectories. However, analysis of candidate trajectories has indicated
      that the majority are feasible. Broad information about the fuel consumption rate
      of the aircraft has been used to inform the range of allowable Mach numbers.
      5. The ﬂuctuations in speed, angle and the error in wind velocity are well-modelled
      by the OU process. The parameters of the OU model were selected to model these
      quantities based on recorded data from real ﬂights.
      6. The random turn and speed change model is rich enough to describe the real
      aircraft dynamics and the implicit preferred path for the model does not bias
      prediction. Validation results in the next chapter show that the model successfully
      produces pdfs containing the true aircraft location

      # Conditional evidence and false precision in Bayesian reconstruction of the final flight of MH370

      Pete Large

      Research-review draft, 18 September 2026. Author details and declarations subject to author approval.

      ## ABSTRACT

      The search for MH370 illustrates the difficulty of estimating a location from sparse observations, uncertain behaviour and heterogeneous evidence. We investigate how satellite measurements, aircraft dynamics, fuel, debris transport and unsuccessful searches change conditional probability distributions, and whether proposed additional observables improve localisation. An integrated computational framework produces explicit alternatives rather than a single assumption-independent search map. Recovered debris and search non-detection substantially redistribute probability, but numerical replication exposes instability in fine-scale search allocations. Pléiades imagery provides a conditional transport constraint without establishing object identity. Antenna-gain and WSPR controls do not demonstrate a reliable additional localisation benefit. Hydroacoustic comparisons identify testable timing relationships but no convincing common-impact association in publication-derived traces. We report 50%, 95% and 99% conditional display regions alongside their limitations. The principal result is an evidence-admission and sensitivity framework: combining more information is valuable only when its likelihood, dependence, physical validity and numerical support are defensible.

      ## 1. INTRODUCTION

      The disappearance of Malaysia Airlines flight MH370 presents an unusual navigation problem. The aircraft was not continuously observed during its final hours, yet satellite communications retained information about its range and motion. Debris subsequently recovered around the western Indian Ocean provides a different, delayed constraint. Extensive unsuccessful seabed searches supply information of another kind: an aircraft lying within a genuinely well-searched area should have had some probability of being detected. These observations concern the same event but have different uncertainties, dependencies and failure modes.

      It is tempting to regard each additional observation type as another line on a map, with their intersection revealing the answer. That interpretation is generally unsafe. A satellite-frequency measurement does not uniquely specify a heading; a recovered object does not follow a uniquely reversible drift trajectory; an unidentified image object is not automatically aircraft debris; and an acoustic transient is not an aircraft detection merely because its timing can be made compatible. The useful question is not whether a plausible reconstruction can be drawn, but how much of the range of plausible reconstructions the evidence actually excludes.

      The satellite timing and frequency analyses described by Ashton et al. (2015) established the central observational framework. Davey et al. (2016) developed Bayesian state estimation and explicitly considered uncertain flight behaviour. Their treatment also recognised recovered debris and unsuccessful searches as potential updates. Our purpose is not to claim invention of this framework or to replace the official investigation. It is to examine, within an inspectable computational implementation, the sensitivity of its outputs to behavioural assumptions, physical approximations, evidence selection and numerical support.

      Two distinctions motivate the study. First, the probability density function (PDF) for an airborne position is not the PDF for the wreckage location. The latter additionally depends on fuel state, the interpretation of the final transmissions and motion until impact. Secondly, a narrow computed PDF need not represent strong information. It can result from a restrictive behavioural prior or a calculation retaining too few distinct trajectories. These possibilities must be separated before a contour becomes a proposed search boundary.

      We therefore ask three questions. How do the available observation types align or conflict when expressed as conditional distributions? Which proposed observables survive controls sufficiently well to justify admission? How reproducible are the resulting broad regions and fine-scale allocations? The study includes positive calculations, negative controls and unsuccessful numerical experiments. Its objective is an estimator using diverse evidence, but only to the extent supported by defensible observation models. Where calibration is incomplete, the result remains a sensitivity analysis rather than an operational recommendation.

      ## 2. METHODOLOGY

      ### 2.1. An accessible view of the estimator

      The calculation begins by generating possible aircraft histories. Each history contains positions, speeds, altitudes, navigation modes and changes of command. Atmospheric and magnetic models connect the commanded state to the resulting motion. At each recorded satellite contact, the predicted timing and frequency are compared with the measurements. Histories receive different weights according to their compatibility, allowing uncertainty to propagate through successive contacts instead of selecting one apparently good route.

      Candidate trajectory simulation uses performance and limitation models for the Boeing 777-200ER, together with the available information about the earlier flight and fuel. The present public-data models are approximations, not a manufacturer-certified performance deck. A candidate that lies within a declared envelope is therefore not automatically a flight that the actual aircraft could have completed. This distinction is retained in the interpretation and in the proposed review tests.

      Cruise inference ends at approximately 00:11 UTC. Weighted histories are then continued through fuel exhaustion, the final satellite transmissions and the modelled descent to impact. Debris likelihoods and unsuccessful-search likelihoods update those impact weights. This modular arrangement preserves the relationship between the airborne history and its impact location, while making it possible to switch evidence sources or physical alternatives explicitly. It also prevents a fuel or satellite constraint from being counted again merely because a later module uses the same trajectory.

      ### 2.2. Probability model and evidence boundaries

      Let h denote a complete aircraft history, x its impact location, and M a specified physical and behavioural model. With satellite data D_s and fuel information D_f, the conceptual distribution is proportional to the history prior multiplied by the joint observational likelihood. The impact distribution follows by propagating those weighted histories through a terminal model. The mathematical structure is standard Bayesian prediction and updating; the substantive choices lie in the prior, the likelihoods and the transition model (Davey et al., 2016, section 3.1).

      For the final-contact comparison, let q_m(x) be the normalised, separately fitted impact distribution under contact case m. Each case initially receives weight a_m = 1/4 at a declared boundary after its satellite fit and common airborne condition. If L_d(x) is the debris likelihood and L_s(x) the unsuccessful-search likelihood, the conditional mixture is

          Z_m = integral q_m(x) L_d(x) L_s(x) dx;
          a'_m = a_m Z_m / sum_k(a_k Z_k);
          p_res(x) = sum_m[a_m q_m(x) L_d(x) L_s(x)] / sum_k(a_k Z_k).     (1)

      Both location and contact-case weights can therefore change. Setting a likelihood to one removes that particular update; it does not establish that the corresponding evidence is uninformative. The drift cases first condition on their available spatial support. Uncomputed support is disclosed separately, not assigned a fabricated zero likelihood.

      Equation (1) is a mixture of conditional estimates, not a calibrated inference about which final BFO is reliable. Normalising constants from fits to different subsets of observations cannot, by themselves, be used as evidence for reliability. A fully generative alternative would model both ordinary and contaminated readings for the same recorded data. Bayesian model averaging requires that comparable-data boundary (Hoeting et al., 1999). Here the equal initial weights are an explicit sensitivity choice, and alternative aerodynamic models remain separate rather than being averaged as if their disagreement were random sampling error.

      ### 2.3. Flight histories, fuel and numerical computation

      The principal broad cruise calculation starts with 1,020,000 candidates across six independently seeded runs of unequal size. Its configured process permits up to sixteen turn starts, eight Mach-command changes and eight altitude-command changes after 18:25. Commanded Mach spans 0.73–0.84 and altitude 25,000–43,000 ft, with finite-rate transitions. True and magnetic heading, true and magnetic track, and lateral navigation are represented. Modes generally persist; the allowed lateral-navigation transition does not amount to unrestricted switching among all modes.

      A shared uncertain mean waiting time controls the turn, speed and altitude clocks. Conditional on that time scale the event processes are separate, but their inferred rates are coupled. Allowing more commands consequently does not remove behavioural assumptions. A person could have flown differently, used a different waypoint sequence or changed modes repeatedly. Those possibilities are not excluded by the observations simply because they are absent from this model.

      Propagation uses World Geodetic System 1984 geometry, UTC contact times, pinned atmospheric fields and magnetic declination. Vertical velocity contributes to the frequency prediction. A shared frequency bias is updated analytically rather than assigning an independent offset to every contact. Sequential importance weighting corrects computational guidance back to the specified physical prior; guidance is not an additional observation. Run records preserve seeds, configuration, input identities, source identities and ancestry.

      Fuel is conditioned on a declared 00:15–00:19 exhaustion window, an earlier remaining-fuel interval and correlated flow-scale uncertainty. The continuation maps that condition back to allowable fuel at 00:11. Neither the timing window nor the public flow proxy is a direct measurement of the terminal fuel state. The calculation applies this information once. A more accurately calibrated aircraft-specific model could change both feasible histories and their relative weights.

      The main refined terminal comparison uses two independently seeded runs, each drawing 12,288 weighted cruise parents, under two public aerodynamic models. It preserves the cruise pool shared by those repetitions. Consequently, terminal replication does not test whether the original cruise distribution has found every important history. Aircraft-state resampling also produces related descendants, so the number of stored rows is not the number of independent posterior samples.

      ### 2.4. Validation and reporting rules

      We distinguish implementation checks, numerical replication and physical validation. Independent calculations of coordinates, likelihood factors and importance corrections can detect software errors. Repeated runs test numerical variability under the same model. Known-flight and generated-data controls test recovery, but neither automatically validates accident-specific fuel, terminal dynamics or the representation of human behaviour. Passing one category cannot substitute for another.

      Probability regions are constructed by ranking density on an equal-area grid. The main maps use 5 km cells and Gaussian display smoothing with a 25 km standard deviation. The 50%, 95% and 99% boundaries are calculated explicitly from this smoothed density; they are not latitude intervals or operationally connected search routes. Reported broad probabilities use the unsmoothed weighted impacts. Grid and smoothing choices are documented because apparent region size can otherwise be mistaken for measurement precision.

      We also evaluate a fixed selected region in an independent terminal run. This is essential when a small set of simulated locations can dominate cell ranking. Selecting and assessing the same cells using the same samples rewards accidental numerical peaks. The independent comparison retains the physical assumptions and evidence conditions, while exposing this source of apparent search efficiency.

      ## 3. OBSERVATION TYPES AND PRELIMINARY ASSESSMENT

      ### 3.1. Satellite timing, frequency and received power

      Burst timing offset (BTO) measures a communications timing residual that constrains range through the satellite and ground-station geometry. Its locus on an assumed altitude surface is an arc, not a point. Burst frequency offset (BFO) contains motion-related Doppler terms, aircraft compensation, satellite and ground-station contributions and an equipment bias (Ashton et al., 2015, equation 2). It helps distinguish histories along and between arcs but is not a direct heading measurement. Wind, speed, vertical motion and compensation assumptions affect its interpretation.

      Aircraft histories must cross successive timing constraints at their recorded times and fit the frequency observations within their error model. Many such histories can remain possible. Their relative density depends on how paths are generated, not merely on whether each path passes a residual threshold. Treating all visually acceptable trajectories as equally probable would replace one prior with another without declaring it.

      Received power may contain an additional directional signature through antenna gain. We examined the hypothesis that a heading-related gain variation remains in the observations, against full precompensation. Earlier MH371 controls held back known positions for scoring and kept non-antenna settings fixed. At the final checkpoint, the two-seed mean posterior-position error decreased from 26.36 to 20.13 nautical miles. However, the smallest highest-density region containing truth increased from approximately 67% to 75%, so improved point localisation did not imply improved density calibration.

      The primary blocked power comparison was not stable across flights or validation designs. Its combined mean log-density difference was 0.116 per point with an event-bootstrap interval spanning zero. A separate early-MH370 chronological holdout favoured no precompensation, with received-power RMSE of 1.294 versus 1.456 dB. These are power residuals, not position errors. The available early-flight states do not supply a defensible blind spatial control across the climb regime. Ground track is also not true heading.

      The gain results therefore support an explicitly conditional sensitivity, not an established extra localisation observable. The historical MH371 implementation is distinguished from the current cruise estimator. Limited events, overlapping training sets and dependent epochs prevent descriptive bootstrap or paired statistics from establishing general detection performance. An antenna pattern can be physically plausible without the proposed effect being demonstrably recoverable from these particular received-power records.

      ### 3.2. Aircraft performance and end of flight

      The final log-on contains two transmissions, here called R600 and R1200, at 00:19:29.416 and 00:19:37.443 UTC. The retained R600 BTO is 18,400 microseconds after its channel correction; the anomalous R1200 timing value is not used. All four contact cases require the transmitting aircraft to remain airborne through the second time and compare neither final BFO, either individually, or both in sequence, without an additional oscillator warm-up offset. Other startup hypotheses are retained as sensitivities, not silently fitted away.

      Low final BFO values can be compatible with rapid descent under specified horizontal motion and equipment behaviour. They do not uniquely establish the impact point, pitch attitude or subsequent control. Flight-path angle is the direction of motion, not necessarily the aircraft body's pitch. The terminal models propagate public drag polars and lift/bank controls; they do not resolve breakup, post-stall aerodynamics or a detailed water-entry process. States that leave the declared model envelope are reported as unresolved outcomes rather than impossible aircraft histories.

      The extended atmosphere grid removes an earlier altitude-coverage exit, but near-vertical and minimum-speed exits remain. The acoustic interpretation inherits these limits because impact timing, velocity and energy depend on the continuation. A separate saved-trajectory performance audit finds some thrust demands above the nominal public proxy, with material differences between drag models. Its selected histories are illustrative, not a probability sample; they cannot justify censoring a corresponding fraction of the posterior.

      This explicit terminal propagation differs from applying a fixed radial offset around an airborne PDF. Davey et al. (2016, section 10.5) described a separate descent kernel; the present implementation attempts a history-dependent continuation. Its added detail is useful for sensitivity and consistency checks, but greater mechanical detail should not be confused with stronger empirical validation.

      ### 3.3. Recovered debris and ocean transport

      Recovered debris supplies a geographically independent class of information, but its interpretation requires transport and recovery models. We use forward simulations from possible source locations rather than reversing individual stochastic paths. A likelihood compares the resulting encounters with reported recoveries, allowing object-dependent wind and wave response. The principal integrated condition uses nine recovered objects with GLORYS12 currents and WAVERYS wave information. Alternative subsets, transport formulations and response parameters are reported separately.

      Recovery position is not necessarily beaching position, and recovery date is not necessarily arrival date. Coastal retention, release, search effort and object identification can all affect the observation process. Multiple objects also share environmental model errors; multiplying their conditional likelihoods does not eliminate those correlations. The scientific question is how strongly a specified transport-and-recovery model distinguishes source locations, not how many trajectories can be drawn backwards to an arc.

      Object-deletion controls identify tension within the debris evidence. The Réunion flaperon favours a more southerly contribution than some recovered cowling/panel observations, notably the Mossel Bay and Paindane cases in the current implementation. Removing these observations changes the update materially. This is a reason to examine response and recovery assumptions, not to discard an inconvenient object after inspecting the desired search location.

      Rare simulated coastal encounters introduce a second uncertainty: the likelihood surface itself may be noisy. Independent cowling calculations improve support but do not remove the full-grid variation. Two transport-simulation repeats are therefore carried into the impact comparison. Isotope interpretations and Australian non-recovery are available as separate declared sensitivities, but are not included in the principal nine-object mixture. Their absence must not be mistaken for a finding that they have no evidential value.

      ### 3.4. Pléiades imagery as a conditional hypothesis

      Geoscience Australia's analysis of Pléiades imagery acquired on 23 March 2014 identified objects considered probably non-natural; it did not identify them as MH370 debris (Minchin et al., 2017). Griffin and Oke (2017) investigated source locations conditional on some image objects having come from the aircraft. We retain that conditional structure. The image analysis and the transport calculation answer different questions: what the objects might be, and where they might have originated if the proposed identity is correct.

      Morphological controls compared aircraft-part masks with matched random geometry and generic ocean objects. The extended experiment included 26,976 masks and 134,880 family–object comparisons. Aircraft shapes remained admissible, but the best matches were not specific to aircraft across the selected objects. An overlap score measures shape agreement after allowed transformations; it is not an identity probability. Post-hoc buoyancy scenarios can demonstrate geometric possibility, but cannot retrospectively supply a calibrated prior for those shapes.

      Forward transport was evaluated under BRAN2016, OSCAR and GLORYS12/WAVERYS alternatives with declared leeway and dispersion. Their modes lie near 35°S and 92–93°E, but pairwise total variation distances are 0.484–0.570 and modes differ by 54–94 km. Their previously reported 90% regions range from approximately 18,400 to 24,300 km². These are conditional source-model outputs, not the 95% residual regions reported below and not three independent measurements of the same source.

      The imagery can consequently motivate a hypothesis-targeted search or a conditional update. It cannot narrow the unconditional distribution unless the probability of the object association and the alternatives are addressed. Recovered-debris and image-transport calculations may also share current-model errors. Their apparent agreement should not be counted twice as independent confirmation. The principal integrated mixture below excludes the image association; its separate source surfaces and coupling methods are supplied for review.

      ### 3.5. Hydroacoustics: information not yet exhausted

      Hydrophone arrays can record low-frequency ocean sound over large distances. Relative arrival times across an array can constrain bearing, while propagation models connect source time and position to reception. Kadri (2024) reported candidate events and discussed the difficulties caused by interference at Diego Garcia. The official search report described an earlier investigated event as probably geological and found no useful search constraint from that analysis (ATSB, 2017, pp. 114–116). Neither finding makes every future hydroacoustic analysis uninformative.

      Our current tests use calibrated vector extractions from published pressure plots, not the raw hydrophone channels. They preserve enough timing and amplitude structure to test some claimed alignments, but cannot reproduce array beamforming, measure a new bearing or calibrate detection probability. The published plots have already undergone filtering and display reduction. Panel edges and unshown time intervals impose real information limits.

      The latest source/timing comparison contains four million computational draws across sixteen banks. It crosses arc or Pléiades spatial conditions with terminal, recovery and extended timing alternatives. Both filtered and unfiltered Diego Garcia traces and nuisance-event exclusions are examined. These alternatives create 64 searches per statistic, not 64 independent observations. Phase-randomised and burst-preserving controls repeat the complete search, with an additional correction for the two statistics tested.

      No added search passes the 5% whole-experiment threshold under both background models. The largest added correlation is approximately 0.282 and is not exceptional after the declared search correction. Some apparently attractive alignments select residual airgun structure or the first usable sample after a panel edge. These results do not reject the impact hypotheses: paired reception coverage is incomplete, propagation and source coupling are uncertain, and the raw channels are unavailable.

      Hydroacoustics therefore remains a potential additional observable rather than an admitted likelihood. Useful next steps are raw-channel access, station and processing metadata, contemporaneous noise characterisation, propagation uncertainty and injection/recovery tests for a pre-declared detector. Known acoustic sources and controlled injections can test components even without a labelled MH370 impact. A convincing association would need to outperform plausible background events across the complete position/time/template search. Non-detection would become disconfirming only after demonstrating a credible probability of detection for the relevant impact family.

      ### 3.6. WSPR and evidence not admitted

      Weak Signal Propagation Reporter (WSPR) records amateur-radio reception reports. An aircraft might perturb a radio signal, but ionospheric variability, transmitter behaviour and receiver effects also influence the records. Intersections of radio-link great circles do not themselves demonstrate aircraft detection. We tested published anomaly flags, frequency residuals with shared station corrections, and a background-calibrated combined score against the known MH371 flight.

      In the latest truth-separated localisation rerun, 24,000 trajectory candidates were scored before the recorded track entered evaluation. The truth reached only the 43.6th score percentile under baseline weighting. Selecting the highest-scoring tenth of baseline probability mass increased weighted mean whole-path RMSE from 150.8 to 291.2 km. An equal-budget feature/scale search left truth near the middle of the score distribution. Previously examining this flight prevents calling the rerun a newly blinded experiment.

      The result is no demonstrated localisation benefit under these models, not proof that high-frequency aircraft sensing is impossible. The analysis lacks raw phase/delay observations, validated propagation modes and complete historical environmental identities for every earlier bank. Dependent trajectory and shifted-track controls do not provide a simple independent-sample significance test. WSPR is consequently excluded from the principal location update. Future admission requires genuinely held-out flights, calibrated propagation and receiver behaviour, and evidence that the signal improves localisation rather than merely fitting anomalies.

      ### 3.7. Unsuccessful seabed searches

      A failed search is an observation only through a detection model. At a location x, the likelihood of no detection is 1 minus the probability of detecting wreckage there, given the search actually conducted. A map outline alone does not supply that probability. Coverage gaps, terrain, sensor performance, target recognisability and repeated passes matter. Multiple campaigns may share blind spots, so their misses are not necessarily independent.

      The implemented search update preserves polygon holes and compares uniform effective-detection values of 50%, 80% and 94% within the detailed ATSB footprint. The 94% reference is an approximation motivated by the official coverage assessment, not a pointwise sonar-quality map (ATSB, 2017, figure 73). An approximate 2018 outline is tested separately at 60%, with shared and independent miss assumptions. A searched location is downweighted rather than automatically removed.

      Published totals from later campaigns do not supply a usable spatial likelihood. The Malaysian update of 8 March 2026 reports approximately 7,571 km² surveyed during the recent operation, but does not give the swath coordinates and quality information needed here. We therefore do not subtract that total from a putative remaining region or label it an unsearched allocation (Ministry of Transport Malaysia, 2026). This distinction is particularly important when discussing an illustrative future 7,500 km² budget: similar numbers need not describe the same area, campaign or evidential condition.

      ## 4. RESULTS

      ### 4.1. Broad flight support and evidence tension

      The broad cruise calculation places approximately 90.1% of its 00:11 airborne probability between 35°S and 38°S, with 8.8% north of 35°S. These are conditional model outputs, not measured coordinates. A separate narrower command process with vertical Doppler disabled places approximately 86.5% in the same band. Because both the physical process and frequency model differ, their contrast cannot isolate a single causal assumption. Recent prior-initialised runs retain the broad regional pattern while still showing non-negligible coordinate-distribution discrepancies.

      Restricting the permitted histories can tighten a region without adding evidence. In an archived matched behavioural selection, 90% airborne area on a 10 km grid decreases from approximately 14,800 km² for the broad history set to 7,700 km² for a selected constant-true-track, at-most-one-turn subset. This is conditional restriction, not an observational discovery or the present residual impact region. It illustrates why a simple control law should be named in a figure caption rather than hidden inside the estimator.

      The integrated contact mixture produces a different question: where is impact after terminal motion, and how does other evidence update it? Figure 1 compares cumulative impact latitude distributions before and after debris and unsuccessful-search evidence. The shift is substantial. Across the two aerodynamic models, terminal repeats and cowling repeats, the nine-object-plus-ATSB94 condition assigns approximately 74–83% north of 35°S. This range describes model and numerical sensitivity, not a confidence interval. It must not be transferred to a different drift subset or campaign mask.

      ![Figure 1](figures/evidence-and-repeatability.png)

      Figure 1. Conditional impact latitude distributions as evidence is added. Rows retain separate aerodynamic models; colours distinguish independent terminal runs and line styles distinguish cowling-drift repeats. All four final-contact cases start with equal weight after their separate satellite fits. The shared cruise pool means these are not fully independent reconstructions. Probabilities are calculated from unsmoothed saved weights.

      The northern redistribution should not be interpreted simply as satellite evidence being wrong or debris evidence being right. It identifies tension between their conditional supports. Strong reweighting may favour a relatively small and numerically fragile part of the cruise pool. Changes in object response, rare-event transport support, exhaustion assumptions or detection effectiveness could alter that balance. The reviewable result is the explicit movement and its causes, not one privileged midpoint between incompatible estimates.

      ### 4.2. Residual 50%, 95% and 99% regions

      Figure 2 shows the principal residual condition: the four-case final-contact mixture, nine recovered objects and the ATSB94 unsuccessful-search update. It displays both aerodynamic alternatives and both refined terminal repeats, keeping cowling repeat A fixed. No Pléiades, acoustic or WSPR association is imposed. The maps are conditional on computed impacts and the modelled drift domain; unresolved terminal outcomes remain outside their normalisation.

      ![Figure 2](figures/residual-50-95-99.png)

      Figure 2. Residual 50%, 95% and 99% highest-density display regions. Each panel is a separate conditional estimate, not one quarter of a pooled PDF. The 5 km equal-area grid is smoothed with a 25 km Gaussian standard deviation. The green outline is the ATSB footprint approximation; the seventh arc uses a reference altitude, not every candidate's actual altitude. Smoothing can extend shading across a footprint boundary; original particle weights determine the search update. These contours are not certified operational search limits.

      The table reports the actual grid areas rather than suggesting a small round-number search zone. Differences between panels are meaningful diagnostics, but a common contour does not establish physical calibration. The 99% region is especially sensitive to weakly supported tails, model-domain limits and smoothing. It cannot be interpreted as a 99% guarantee that an aircraft with arbitrary unmodelled behaviour lies inside the displayed
